Rayleigh scattering is a fundamental optical phenomenon in which light or other electromagnetic radiation is scattered by particles much smaller than the wavelength of the radiation. It is the dominant mechanism responsible for the blue color of the daytime sky, the reddish hue of sunsets, and the blue appearance of the eyes of many animals. Understanding this process requires examining the interaction between electromagnetic waves and the induced polarization of individual molecules, and it provides one of the most elegant demonstrations of how the microscopic structure of matter governs macroscopic visual experience.

**Physical Mechanism**

When an electromagnetic wave propagates through a medium composed of molecules, the oscillating electric field of the wave exerts a force on the electrons within each molecule. Because the electrons are bound to the nuclei but possess some degree of freedom, they are displaced slightly from their equilibrium positions, creating a time-varying electric dipole moment. This induced dipole, in turn, radiates electromagnetic energy in all directions—a process known as dipole radiation. The scattered wave has the same frequency as the incident wave (elastic scattering) but is redistributed in direction and, critically, in intensity as a function of wavelength.

For Rayleigh scattering to be the appropriate description, two conditions must be satisfied. First, the scattering particles must be significantly smaller than the wavelength of the incident light, typically with a size parameter x = 2πr/λ well below unity. In the atmosphere, nitrogen (N₂) and oxygen (O₂) molecules, with diameters on the order of 0.3 nanometers, scatter visible light (wavelengths 400–700 nm) under this condition. Second, the molecules must be much smaller than the coherence length of the incident radiation, ensuring that the scattering is coherent within each molecule but incoherent between molecules, so that the total scattered intensity is the sum of individual molecular contributions rather than a coherent sum.

**The Inverse Fourth-Power Law**

The most important quantitative result of Rayleigh scattering theory is the dependence of scattered intensity on wavelength. For a single isotropic molecule with polarizability α, the differential scattering cross-section is given by:

dσ/dΩ = (k⁴ α² / 4π ε₀²) × (1 + cos²θ) / 2

where k = 2π/λ is the wavenumber, θ is the scattering angle, and ε₀ is the vacuum permittivity. The crucial factor is k⁴, which means the scattered intensity is proportional to 1/λ⁴. A photon of wavelength 450 nm (blue) is therefore scattered approximately (650/450)⁴ ≈ 4.3 times more strongly than a photon of wavelength 650 nm (red). This steep wavelength dependence is the direct mathematical origin of the sky's color.

The angular dependence (1 + cos²θ) indicates that scattering is symmetric about the forward and backward directions and is strongest at 90° relative to the incident beam. This is why the sky appears brightest at a right angle to the Sun's position.

**Why the Sky Is Blue and Not Violet**

Given the 1/λ⁴ dependence, one might expect the sky to appear violet, since violet light (around 400 nm) is scattered even more strongly than blue light (around 450 nm). Several factors conspire to produce the perceived blue color rather than violet:

1. **Solar spectrum**: The Sun's blackbody-like emission (approximately 5800 K) contains less violet and ultraviolet radiation than blue and green. The solar flux at 400 nm is roughly half that at 450 nm.

2. **Atmospheric absorption**: Ozone in the stratosphere absorbs a significant fraction of ultraviolet and near-violet radiation (the Hartley and Huggins bands), reducing the amount of violet light available for scattering.

3. **Human photopic vision**: The human eye's three cone types (S, M, L) have peak sensitivities at approximately 420 nm, 534 nm, and 56
